to note that the monarch is here referred to 
by his personal name in addition to his viruda. 
Among the numerous epigraphs 
of this ruler, there is only one other inscription 
3 which mentions him. by his 
name, all the rest ·using only the title of Sirisangbo. 
The object of the record was to proclaim certain rules concerning a' Water 
Pavilion' (pan ma¢iya) at the Ratnamapirivena 
• in the J etavana monastery.

seem to ha\·e, rather too frequently, made use of the monastic buildings as temporary residences. The

seems to have been a building near the entrance to a large mansion, where

8 Ui•asu = P. upiisaka. 
Originally, this word meant any lay adherent of the Buddhist creed in 
contrast to a bhz"kkhu, one who had adopted the life of a religious recluse. 
In the tenth-century 
inscriptions, the terms upiisaka and uvasu are applied to laymen who resided in the monasteries to

monastery, encroaching on the grounds of the l\lahavihara. 
(See 111v., ch. xxxvii, vv. 32-9, and the 
Nil<li)'a Saizgraha, Wickremasinghe's edition, p. 15.) 
He held views on religion opposed to those of 
the Mahavihara brethren and was publicly charged for a grave offence and excommunicated by the 
orthodox monks. 
In remarkable contrast to the flattering language applied to him in this inscription, 
the chroniclers of the l\Iahavihara delight in heaping opprobrious epithets whenever they refer to Tissa. 
The .¥ahiivamsa 
calls him 'a hypocrite, a plotter, the lawless' and' evil friend' (ch. xxxvii, v. 32). 
The later Sinhalese chronicles, 1he Pii}ii7Jali and Nikiiya Samgraha, invariably attach to his name the 
sobriquet of kohon (P. kuhana) 'the hypocrite'. 
Perhaps, Tissa was not so black as he was painted 
by the orthodox chroniclers; and there is no doubt that he suffered on account of the sectarian bigotry 
of his opponents.

1 Siiguli, S.igali, or Siigala was the name of a lhera who was the leader of a separatist movement 
from the Dhammaruci sect which had its head-quarters at the Abhayagiri Vihara. 
He flourished in 
the reign of Go~haka Abhaya and after his secession from the Dhammarucikas, he took up his abode at 
the Dakkhil).agiri Vihara. 
His followers, known as the Sagaliyas, formed one of the three contending 
parties of the Buddhist Church in Ceylon during the Anuradhapura period. 
The JJfahiithera Sagaliya 
is spoken of with respect by the author of the Nikiiya Sangraha, perhaps owing to the opposition that 
he showed towards the Dhammarucikas; 
but when his followers began to exert an independent 
influence, founded a rival monastery and gained royal patronage, they were equally obnoxious to the 
monks of the 1\lahavihara as the Dhammarucikas. 
For an account of the lJfahiithera Sagali and his 
sect, see .Nikiiya Sangraha, English translation, p. 1 3.

HE slab pillar_ on.~hi~h this ins~ripti~n- i~ cut ori?inally _stood in ~~nde-
vatta or Kande D1sanayaka Mud1yanselage-vatta 
situated m Pamunetanna, 
a-hamlet of Kevulgama village in Gampaha Kora!e \Vest of U½la Dumbara in 
Kandy District. 
Some thirty years ago the pillar was blasted in search of hidden 
treasure by the then owner shortly after the death of his father, who, it is said, 
regarded the record as of great value and actually slept by its side on a rock-
hewn bed. 
The upper and main part of the pillar was then removed to the 
local vihare by the priest and built into the inner doorway of the temple. In this 
position, wherein three lines of the inscription at the top and two at the bottom 
were concealed by the masonry, it was copied by the Archeological Survey in 
1909; it then measured 
2 ft. 4 in. by I ft. 2 in. Recently the writer induced the 
priest to consent to the excavation of the record; the remaining part also was 
brought by the priest from its original site to the pansala below the temple. 
The inscription consists of eighteen lines on the front of the pillar and is 
continued in nine lines on the right-hand side under a crescent moon; on the 
left-hand side is the figure of the sun. 
The fragments of some five ak1aras on 
the lower portion of the pillar form the continuation of line 18; thus by a lucky 
chance the blasting operation has done no irreparable damage. 
The letters on 
the front cover a space of 3 ft. I} in. in length ; they vary from 1 ¾ to 1 ! in. in 
height. 
The record is dated in the first year of King Siri Saftgabo Ka.li:nga Vijaya 
Bahu. 
There 
were three rulers of the name Kalinga 
Vijaya Bahu. 
In 
E. Z., Vol. II, No. 36, pp. 2, 9 ff., Sahasa Malla is so styled with the prefix 
Siri Sangabo, precisely as in the present document. 
According to the Nikiiya 
San._r:rahava, Magha also bore the name ; his throne name is not recorded. 
Last 
of all Vijaya Bahn I I I appears in the Rajaratniikaraya 
under the name; he was 
a descendant of Siri Sangabo. 
The script suits any of these princes. 
There 
is no definite proof that Vijaya Bahu III ever held U½la Dumbara, which is 
described as being in Pihiti or the King's Country. 
Magha was a Kalinga 
invader and oppressed the Sinhalese. 
There remains Sahasa Malla. If the 
attribution 
is correct, the date of the inscription is October 
IO, A. D. 1200.

The document is of interest in that it records a grant to one Gulpiti 
or 
Gulhiti But Pambul-le-daruvan 
for loyalty to His Majesty even to the receiv-
ing of wounds in his service. 
The occasion may have been when the two 
ministers. who put Sahasa Malla on the throne, 'in the course of two years sub-
dued the· evil ministers who were causing obstruction with the object of gaining 
personal power for themselves and so were not desiring to have kings that would 
be powerful enough both for (granting) rewards and (for inflicting) punishments 
and that would protect the people and the religion' (E. Z., foe. cit., p. 228). The 
personal name of the grantee represents either Buddha or Bhuta (cf. Mv. Iv, 26, 
lxxii, 196 ). The fact that there is a hamlet Galahi tiya va ir1 Kevulgama suggests 
that the word preceding the personal name should be read as Galhiti. 
But the 
first ak$ara clearly is ~; the third is 8 or possibly tB; the fourth can only be 5 
or~-
The title Pambul-le-daruvan 
may be compared with Dev~ padamz7la-daraka 
in J/ ah,rva,j1sa, lx\·i. 66. 
Geiger renders this by • the son of a royal servant' 
and ·states that padamfi!a 
in the sense of servant appears frequently 
in the 
J atakas. 
The word pa.mu! or pa111bul is still used in the expression pamul- or 
paifzbu!-pe//i'ya, a box or chest which, as containing objects of the greatest value, 
is kept 'at the feet', that is in the innermost room of the house. 
Panzbul-le 
here, therefore, may mean 'trusty', 
or 'confidential 
secretary'. 
In literature 
pamul-pe//iya occurs in an obscure passage of the Da(ada Sirita (ed. E. S. Raja-
sekhara, Kandy, 
1920, 
p. 58) dealing with the replacing of the Tooth Relic in 
its shrine at the end of the procession in which it had been carried; 
it apparently 
refers to the chest in which the Relic Casket or the jewellery belonging to it 
was kept. 
In the Dambaden£ Asna, however, is found: pamul pettiyen pa{ji 
ka si°(ina Simha{a senangin si7-visi lak$a pas-visi da!tasak ltii mese-ma parfi ka 
si/ina Dema{a senal'tgin do{os dahasak ha,' Twenty-four lacs twenty-five thousand 
men of the Sinhalese host drawing pay from the pamul chest and twelve thousand 
of the Tamil host drawing pay in like manner'. 
The office held by the grantee 
thus may have been analogous to that of Keeper of the Privy Purse. 
The 
J\TiJ.:iiJ•a 
Saizgraha.:a attributes to Parakrama Bahu I the institution or reorgani-
zation of the Four Treasuries (sataramuda!a). 
With le-daruvan is to be compared 
kamtan le-daruvak in the 1\!ihintale Tablets (E. Z., Vol. I, p. 1 I I, n. 9). 
Our 
inscription suggests that Deva in the passage of the Mahii.vamsa is a personal name 
and that daraka is an honorific. 
A, lines I 2-14. 
Me vanu mut-hasa pa/an. 
So the Vaharakgoc;la inscription, 
\line 19 (Bell, Report on the Kegalla District, p. 82 and plate), as checked by me

from the rock. 
Taking mut-hasa as the equivalent of mudu-hasa, I tentatively 
translate thus: 'from the time of sealing with the signet (the document) similar 
to this', that is the original of the grant recorded on the stone. 
A, lines 14-16. 
Pilzi# rajaye bada 1/IIandivak Samvalle. 
In the fourteenth 
century Malza Kaefa-im pota Dumbara was reckoned as in Maya rata, the country 
of the Mahaya or Heir Apparent. 
But this was not the case in the twelfth 
century, for in Parakrama Bahu's campaign against Gaja Bahu Majjhimavagga 
or Madivaka was under the direct government of the king (il£v. lxx. 21); 
the 
present inscription is conclusive on this point against Geiger's note I in Ctt!avamsa, 
p. 289. 
The village of Madivaka lies some four miles to the north-east of 
Kevlugama. 
It is said to have been of much greater extent in ancient times 
than at present; in the eighteenth century it gave its name to Gampaha Korale, 
which was known as Madivak Gampaha. 
A village called Hathvalla is situated 
in Gandeke Kora!e at a greater distance from Kevulgama than Madivaka and in 
a separate valley. 
It is possible that our Sarhvalla may refer to land at the 
'junction' 
of the Gang0<;la Oya with the Maha Oya, the main stream running 
through the valley in which Kevulgama lies. 
As to the word itself, it may be 
noted that in Badulla there are five g·ocfa-val or stretches of high land, five mulu 1 
or tongues of paddy fields running up into the high land, and five vali or low-
lying sandy land on the banks of the river, and that of the last named one, at 
the junction of two streams, is called Harhvalla 2• The name of the field Valima<;la 
(' sandy mud land') itself lends some support to this theory. 
But the Harhvalla 
in Gandeke Kora)e possessed a royal granary under the Kandyan government; 
if Madivaka with Harhvalla was a royal village in 1200 
A.D., the land given in 
our inscription may well have been an appurtenance thereof. 
A, line I 7. Liyatda, the modern liyadda, now a small division of a paddy 
field bounded by ridges for the purpose of retaining water. 
Here, however, it 
appears to be identical with 'field' (B, lines 2, 3). 
A, line I 8. 
The restoration within brackets is almost certain. 
B, lines 3, 4. Mi(nisu)n. 
There are traces of the two akfaras within brackets 
on the stone. This word occurs in the grant of the third year of Vikrama Bahu I I I, 
rehearsed at length in the Larhkatilaka copper plates: Rabbegamuva ha mehi 
bada gama mudal 3 gasa kola valpi/a minisun atulu-vu siyallat. The corresponding 
sentence in the grant of Rajadhirajasirhha 
in the same plates is: Rabbegamuva

nzinisun at the date of our inscription perhaps is rendered better by 'serfs ' than

translated · the plantations of the serfs·. 
B. lincs 5, 6. V,d pi/i. These words have been rendered tentatively' the wood 
land and the grass land', that is jungle and open land. 
At present val-pittaniya 
is in use with the meaning of' natural grass land', val being any wild growth of 
the size of grass and upwards. 
But in the fifteenth century valpi/a or val was 
identical with ratmasara and must be the 'waste' land used for chena cultivation. 
In the twelfth century val has this meaning, as well as that of' waste' or jungle 
as opposed to the cultivated gama, and this last meaning is found also in the 
thirteenth century 1• Pi{i-bim at a date earlier than that of our inscription appear 
as appurtenances (cf. E. Z .. Vol. I, No. 16; Vol. II, Nos. 12 and 37), and may 
still have been so in 1200 
A.D. 
B, lines 6. 7. Pamu1p1, the highest form of holding by persons or bodies. 
The memory of the present grant survives in the name of the hamlet Pamune-
tanna, where the slab pillar inscription once stood. 
This grant to a private individual with its rehearsal of his act of valour may 
be compared with the Ihala Puliyankulama pillar containing the stuti of an officer 
of Parakrama Ba.hu I ~.

TRANSLATION. 
2 35 
On the first day of the waxing moon of Hila in the first year of His Majesty 
the Sovereign Lord Siri Sa:ngabo Kali:iiga Vijaya Bahu, for valour shown by 
the most trusty secretary Gulpi(ti) But, (even) for the wounds which he got in 
loyalty to the king from the weapons (of the king's enemies), there was given (to 
him), having been made a pamu~ttt holding and enrolled, from the time of sealing 
with the signet the counterpart of this, (all) \\1ithin the four pillars set up on 
the land appurtenant to Valimac;la liyatda in Mandivak 
Sarhvalla of the Pihiti 
kingdom (to wit) the field, the serfs and the plantations, the wood land and the 
grass land. 
On (this) manner was (the grant made).

HIS inscription has once been published in Sinhalese 1 •. 
But the splendid 
condition of the record, its historical interest, and the absence of any speci-
. men of the script of the period from Epziraphia Zeylanica are sufficient reasons 
for its appearance here. 
It is cut in bold letters on a rock m the village of 
I Visva Brahma9a vamsa vi9iscaya, PP· 81, 82.

[VOL. Ill 
Mac;Iavala in the Pata Dumbara 
division of Kandy District. 
It is enclosed on 
three sides by a rectangular 
frame ; outside of this at the top are the sun and 
moon, a hammer and an anvil, while on the left side are a hammer, an anvil and 
a pair of pincers in the upper half, and a crow and a dog in the lower. 
The inscription records a grant of land made to a silversmith Parama:r;i.aya 
of Siddavulla 
and his son Suriya by King Siri Sangabo Sri Parakrama 
Bahu VI in the year following the forty-sixth of his reign in the presence of 
Dantota-vature 
Devan and Diva:r;i.avatte Lamka Adhikarin. 
The signatures 
of these dignitaries are reproduced in the left-hand upper corner of the record as 
Apa1Ja and 7 oti Si/ii1Ja. The---last named in all probability is the ruler of the 
hill country, whose rebellion 
is thus 
described 
in the R.ti.Ja:;.1ali;•a 
(e<l. B. 
Gunasekara, 
1900, p. 69) :-
' King Sri Parakrama 
Bahu had reigned as a crowned monarch for fifty-two 
years, when the Situ king Sojata 
(@e:iid'o~ 
8~ 612), who was reigning in the 
hill-country, ceased supplying labourers, neglected payment of his yearly tribute, 
and rebelled. 
The king, on hearing of this, raised a large army and committed 
it to the young prince who was at Ambulugala, giving him permission to conquer 
the hill-country. 
'Accordingly 
the prince of Ambulugala 
attacked 
the hill-country and con-
quered it; took captive several relations of the king of the hill-country, and sent 
them down to Kotte. 
The hill-country was entrusted 
to the royal prince of the 
Solar race, descendant of the Gampola dynasty, and to his ministers, and tribute 
was recovered.' 
The poem Parakumbii Sirita, vv. 48-50, gives the following account of these 
events:-
Verse 48. 
'When the ray of the fourfold host of Parakrama 
the lord of 
day like unto the solar king was shining, the thick darkness called Jotiya Situ, 
ruler of men, fled in fear to the confines of the other world. 
Verse 49. 
The swan king, the Apa, rubbed in the pollen of the very red 
lotus, to wit the sacred feet of king Parakrama, the pride of poets, in fame the 
king of the gods, descended from the race of the Sun, extinguished 
the sorrow 
of his mind. 
Verse 50. The Gampola prince quenched his heat, immersed in the precious 
river of the rays of light from the nails of the sacred feet of this king, who is of th·e 
, colour of the moon, pure, having a flood of fame, splendid as the sun, pleasing 1•

The evidence of this contemporary work proves that Gunasekara's 
text is 
corrupt, and that the name of the rebel chief should be read as J otiya and 
not as Soja.ta ; indeed in Valcntyn's version of the Rajavaliya it appears as 
'] ottia Sitti •. Our inscription shows that this ruler held the rank of Larhka 
Adhikarin and came from Divaryavatta, now Jivaryavatta, a village in Harispattuva; 
the title Sz'/it raju is represented 
here by Si/ii~ta. It seems possible that the 
Apa, Dantota-vature 
Devan, is the Gampola prince, whom Parakrama Bahu VI 
put in charge of the hill-country on the defeat of J oti Sitarya. 
If so, at the time 
of our inscription the effective government of the country may have been in the 
hands of the Larhka Adhikarin nominal precedence being accorded to the Apa, 
a member of the royal family. 
Danture in Yatinuvara appears in the N am-pota 
under the name Dantota-vatura. 
A prince of this place, Danture 
Turyaya 
Baryc;lara, is mentioned in the Siduruvana:ra/a Ba~ufaravaliya. 
The accession of Parakrama 
Bahu VI took pla.ce on the full moon day of 
Vesak, 1955 A.B. (1412 A-D) or shortly afterwards. 
Accordingly the date of 
the grant, the full moon of Vesak in his forty-seventh 
year, fell in April, 
1458 A.D., or in April, 1459 A.D. 
For the chronology of the reign, see pp. 52, 
and 53, of this volume. 
The script is that of the period. 
The form of ® in line 8, of e in lines 9 
and I 8 and of i in lines 12 and 13 may be noticed. 
The orthography is not free from errors. 
These will be recorded in the 
footnotes. 
The form kubura still occurs in Kandyan usage. 
The expression gam siiliisma is found in the following documents of the 
Kotte period :-
(i) Papiliyana (Journal, Ceylon Branch, R.A .S., vii (3), 1882). Be!tgal 
nuvara bada bulat-gam siilasmen Pi!(agama. 
(ii) Kurunagala 
District, registered 
number 653. 
Bat-gam saliisme pavata 
a Daih/Jadeni korafaya bada M adagocfa yana gama. 
(iii) Opata Maravala copper plate. 
Vaf allavi!i korafaya bada gam siilasmen 
pafamu J>a!an mese-ma meku-/a prave1J,i-va pavata ena Opata Marava/ayayi yana 
gama-/a him. 
(iv) Oruvala (p. 66 of this volume). 
Amutu-va venin gam salasmak niyama 
kara. 
Salii.sma is 'making', 
'arrangement·, 
'settlement', 
'allocation', 
'plan', 
'lay-out', 
'area'; 
its meaning is somewhat vague. 
I have rendered the .(am 
saliismen of our grant by 'from the village land', namely the area set apart for 
the villages. 
But its precise meaning is not clear. 
It may indicate village land

as opposed to waste, gama as against val; or, perhaps with more probability, in 
view of (i) and (ii), the later gam-vasam land, the villages remaining over after 
the allotment to the military and other departments, 
as opposed e.g. to the 
bulat-gam and bat-gam, the modern gabarfa~r;am or vidana-gam. 
It is possible 
that the use of the word sdldsma implies some such process as entry in the 
lekam-mi/iya or land-register of the province or district. 
Val-pi/a regularly occurs in the grants of the period often coupled with 
vil-pi/a. 
The termination seems to have little or no significance at this time, 
both words being replaced by val and vil; 
it is still used in hznne-pi/a and 
survives in such village names as Valpita and Henpita, 
In the stone-inscription 
at Papiliyana of the forty-fourth year of Parakrama Bahu VI touching Del).avaka 
Vihare we find : Jl,,.firisagala-kande val-pi/a ha. 
Its counterpart 
in another 
document of the same year, surviving only in manuscript, is: ratmasara-va 
Mudalz-vacfuvii-/a sip, Mi[ri]sagala-kande val-pi/a hii. Val-pi/a, therefore, is the 
equivalent of ratmiisara, the later ratmahara, the jungle land in which chena 
cultivation was practised. 
A possible etymology for this last word is raja-maha-
sara, 'the king's great wood'. 
Gocfa-val-pi/a bim are mentioned in the Sabara-
gamu Disii Lekam-mi/iya in the village Nahalvatura 
in Atakalan Korale, and 
gotfa-val-pitz'-karayo in Opalla in the same division 1• 
Who the ten Aghampag.is of Dumbara were is not clear. 
But from the 
use of the word in the Nikaya Samgrahava and in the Niyangampaya 
inscrip-
tion of Vikrama Bahu II I there can be no doubt that they were military persons.

T
HE copper plate described below is in the possession of the Palkumbura 
Vihare in Harispattuva of the Kandy District. 
In its present fragmentary 
condition it measures 9½ by 3½ inches. 
It obviously has suffered badly from the 
action of fire. 
On the left-hand side of the obverse are two double rectangular 
frames; the upper encloses the sun, while in the lower doubtless was the moon. 
On. the reverse the sole remaining symbol is a chank shell, the emblem of Vi~l)U. 
The royal signature is illegible.

The history of the succession at this Vihare is given in the fine copper plate 
granted by the last king of Kandy Sri Vikrama Rajsairhha, on Monday the 
seventh of the waxing moon of Durutu in the Saka year 1726. 
It is there 
rehearsed that while Bhuvanaika Bahn was reigning in KoHe he had one of his 
brothers admitted to the priesthood. 
This Thero, also known as Bhuvanaika 
Bahu, resided at the capital, but on the death of the king migrated to the hill 
country with several of his brothers and settled down at U rulavatta in U c;lunu-
vara. 
During the sojourn of the priest there Vikrama Bihu, the founder of 
Senkac;lagala 1 or Kandy died and was succeeded by his son, Jayavira Asthana, 
who summoned the priest to the city, bade him transcribe the Visuddhimargga 
and as a reward therefor gave him the village of Palkumbura. 
The priest was 
succeeded 
by his nephew, also known as Bhuvanaika 
Bahu Thero. 
This 
nephew, however, during the persecution 
of Buddhism 
by Rajasirhha I of 
Sitavaka disrobed himself but continued to possess Palkumbura under the name 
of Bhuvanaika Bahu Paryc;lita ~!udiyanse and secured the favour of king Senarat, 
whose tutor he was. 
His successor was his nephew, Bhuvanaika Bahu Thero, 
who built a new vihare. 
Further details of the succession are not given, but 
under Sri Vikrama the priest in charge of the vihare showed the king 'the sanhasa 
which from old was at the Palkumbura Vihare and which was charred and half 
burnt with fire' and obtained a fresh dedication together with a new copper 
plate. 
The identity of the king Bhuvanaika 
Ba.hu of KoHe mentioned in the 
copper plate of Saka 1 726 is certain. 
The nephew of the original grantee of 
Palkumbura 
disrobed himself under Rajasirhha I, who was in possession of 
Kandy from about 15 79 to 1591, and became the tutor of Senarat ( 1604-35 A.o.). 
The king of Kotte, therefore, must be Bhuvanaika Ba.hu VJt 
In the penultimate portion of the Mahavamsa, cap. xcii, compiled in the 
reign of Kirttisri, it is stated that Vira Vikkama of t!1e race of Siri Sarhghabodhi 
became king in Seiikharyc;lasela Sirivac;lc;lhanapura in the Buddhist year 2085 
expired (1542-3 A.o.), and that he was a contemporary of Mayadunne 
and 
a zealous Buddhist; it was he who built the Poya Maluva in Kandy. 
This ac-
count is an abbreviation of that given in the Rajaratnakaraya. 
In that work, 
however, the name of the king is not given; from the allusive epithets employed

it may have been Vira Vikrama 
or Vikrama 
Bahu. 
Tradition 
attested 
by 
eighteenth-century 
documents ascribes the foundation of Kandy as the capital 
to Vikrama Balm; the Dambulla Vihare turfapata assigns this event to the 
Buddhist year 2085. 
Tradition 
likewise identifies the builder 
of the Poya 
l\Taluva with the founder of Asgiri Vihare, the Vikrama Balrn who made Kandy 
his capital. 
This Vikrama Bahu is sometimes confused with his namesake of 
Gampola in the fourteenth century. 
In Kandy there seem to have been two kings of the name; one, Siri Sangabo 
Scnasammata Vikrama Uahu, the other, the father of J a ya vira and grandfather 
of Dona Catharina, the princess who married Vimala Dharma Suryya I in 1594. 
Inscriptions of the first Vikrama Bahu exist at Alutnuvara and at Gac;laladei:iiya; 
the Kobbakac;luva Vihare sannasa is dated in his thirty-seventh 
year, and the 
Galgane Vihare grant of no regnal year on the eighth of the waxing moon of 
Vesak, 2052 A.B. (1510 AD.). 
The last two documents exist only in copies. The 
GacJaladei:iiya record is immediately followed by one written in the time of' our 
great king J aya[ vi]ra' on the first of the waning moon of Nikini in 2054 A.B. 
{ I 5 I 1 A.D.). 
Senasammata 
Vikrama Bahu thus seems to have reigned at least 
from 1473-4 to 1510. As the Galgane grant was given in the Sirivardhana 
Palace of Senkhai:ic;lapura, it is to be presumed that it was this king who founded 
Kandy as the capital. 
The identity of the 'Vikrama 
Bahu ', said in the Palkum bura copper plate to 
have died after the mu_rder of Bhuvanaika Bahu VI I in 1551, and of his son and 
successor 'J ayavira Asthana' 
is not clear. 
It seems, however, fairly certain that 
this Vikrama Bahu did not make Kandy the capital, in spite of the statement to 
that effect in the copper plate just mentioned, for this town already was the seat 
of government 
in 15 IO. 
There is, therefore, a confusion between two kings of 
the same name. 
.As to J ayavira Asthana it will be convenient to bear in mind 
what the Rajavaliya has to say about the only ruler of Kandy so designated 
therein. 
From this chronicle (ed. B. Gunasekara) 
we learn that, when Vijaya 
Balm VII Qf Kone proposed to disinherit his sons, one of them, Mayadunne, 
went to the hill country to get help from J ayavira Bai:ic;lara, this ruler's wife 
b~ing related to Mayadunne 
as an 'elder sister', and that with the troops so 
obtained he and his brothers dethroned and murdered their father (pp. 74..:.6). 
This even.t took place in 1521. Later o~ J ayavira Bal).c;lara offended his s011, 
Karalliyadde 
K umara Bai:i<;lara, by his second marriage ; he was expelled from 
.his kingdom, took refuge with Maya.dunne, and is no more heard of (p. 82). 
Karalliyadde in his turn was dethroned by Rajasithha of Sitavaka and died shortly

afterwards, leaving as his heir his infant daughter, Dona Catharina (pp. 89, 97). 
The seizure of the Kandyan kingdom by Rajasirhha took place about I 579. 
The Rajavali_ya account of J ayavira Bal).qara is borne out by th': contem-
porary documents recently published by Schurhammer (Ceylon, Documents 29, 
79, 121 ). 
One and the same king of Kandy was reigning as early as July 1542 
and was still on the throne in January 155 r. 
In 1546 he was 60 years old and 
his son 20. 
The king's name is nowhere mentioned, but from the pedigree 
given in Document 79 there can be little doubt that he is identical with the 
Jayavira of the Sinhalese chronicle. 
V./e also know from the Kandy Natha 
Deva.le inscription that Sri J aya.vira Maha vaqa-vun-tana was on the throne in 
the month of Bak at the end of the Buddhist year 208 5 ( 1 543 A. D. ). 
The Rajasiha S£rita, written in the reign of Rajasirhha II, son of Dona 
Catharina, states that this monarch was the 'grandson' 
(mu1J,uburu) of King 
J ayavira, who was the son of King Vikrama Bahu. 
If we adhere to the Raja• 
va/z"ya pedigree and take the word mu~iuburu in its primary sense of' grandson ' 
and ?0t in that of' descendant', we can only conclude that the J ayavira of 1543 
was also known as Vikrama Bahu. 
There is no evidence for this; yet Kandyan 
tradition consistently places Vikrama Bahu, described as the founder of Kandy, 
in the middle of the sixteenth century and as often as not attaches to him the 
date 2085 A.B., which, as we have seen, appears in the Natha Deva.le inscription. 
The problem at present is unsolved, but there is some slight indication that there 
were two kings, and not one, after J ayavira, and that it was the second of these who 
was dethroned by Rajasirhha I. If this prove to have been the case, Karalliyadde 
may have taken the name of Vikrama Bahu on his accession to the throne, and 
his successor that of J ayavira. 
If, on the other hand, the received pedigree is 
sorrect, Rajasi1hha II must have been mu1J,uburu of the Jayavira of 1543 in the 
word's not uncommon sense of' descendant'. 
We do not know, however, the relationship between Senasammata Vikrama 
Bahu and this Jayavira or whether the last named prince is identical with 
Vikrama Bahu's successor, the J ayavira of 151 I. 
It seems possible that he is 
not identical. 
According to the Rajavaliya (p. 72) the king of KoHe, Dharma 
Parakrama 
Bahu IX, having heard that the king ruling the hill country had 
ascended the throne and refused tribute, sent the prince Sri Rajasirhha to reduce 
him to subjection. 
The Kandyan ruler was forced to sue for peace and gave 
his daughter to wife to Kiravalle Ralahami, Sri Rajasirhha's brother-in-law and 
general. 
It was the daughter of this princess who married the J ayavira of 1543. 
Kiravalle's campaign is placed by the chronicler some time before the murder of

Vijaya Bahu VI I. 
This took place in 152 r, and, as Parakrama Balm IX came 
to the throne in r 509, it seems likely that the new Kandyan king who refused 
tribute, is the J ayavi"ra who succeeded Vikrama Bahu at some date between 
Vesak of 15 r o and Nikini of 1 5 r I. 
If so, he cannot be the same as the J a yavira 
of 1 543, who married his granddaughter. 
Even if the text of the Rajiivaliya 1 
can be interpreted as indicating the rebellion of a vassal king already on the 
throne and not the accession of a new ruler, it can hardly refer to Vikrama Bahu, 
as he always speaks of himself as Cakravartti. 
To sum up, of one fact only is 
there certainty; in no case can the date 2085 A.B. fit in with the accession of any 
Vikrama Bahu. The Natha Deva.le record is decisive on this point. This temple 
is comm,mly attributed to Vikrama Bahu,-the founder of Kandy, and the date, 
208 5 A.B., so often ascribed to him, may well have been taken from the inscription 
itself. 
The charred plate according to the Kandyan documents was the 'sanhasa 
which from old was at the Palkumbura Vihare ·. It manifestly was not the copper 
plate grant of that Vihare, as it refers to a temple or to land situated at Divigoc;la 
in the Galu Kora1aya in the present Southern Province ; a village Digo~la exists 
in the Talpe Pattuva. 
The script of this copper plate is that employed at Kone in the sixteenth 
century and closely resembles that of the Nakolagane inscription of 2101 
A.B., 
reproduced in Plate 4 of this volume. 
It is dated in the reign of Siri Saiigabo 
Sri Bhuvanaika Bahu, the seventh of the name, and records a grant of land, 
subject to a payment to Vi!jl).U, to the Maha Thero of the Senevirat-mu.la 
establishment. 
The Vrtta miilii of the reign of Parakrama Balm V mentions 
Senapati-mu1a Thero; Larhka Senevirat Piruvan Terasamin, pupil of Vilgammula 
Sarhgharaja, was the author of the Vimukti Samgraha under Vikrama Bahu II I. 
The name of the attesting 
authority 
may have been Nayinaru; 
Sivatta 
N ayinaru was the corresponding official of Senasammata 
Vikrama Bahu of 
Kandy.

TWO ROCK-INSCRIPTIONS 
FROM 
LABUA TAB.ANDI GALA. 
(A. S. J. Nos. 449 and 450.) 
By s. PAitANAVITANA. 
JABUATABANDIGALA
1 is a rock situated about one and a half miles to 
L 
the north-east of Morava.va, a village in the Kalpe Korale of the North-
Central Province. 
On this rock there is a ruined stupa, a few yards to the south 
of which the two epigraphs dealt with in this paper are incised. 
Inscription No. 449 covers an area of about 6 ft. by I ft. 10 in., and consists 
of five lines of writing of which the last has only five letters preserved. 
The 
auspicious word siddham is written to the left somewhat apart from the main 
1 This place was visited by Mr. H. C. P. Bell in 1892. 
In his Annual Report for that year (p. 7) 
he gives the following brief note about the site:-' 
Labuetagala :-A 
flat rock reach, 1½ miles from 
Morav~wa on the pinpara to Mahakapugoll~va. Two inscriptions, a good deal worn. 
The name of the 
king is not given; but the name of the ancient rock temple "Chevagiri Vihiirl" (sic) occurs in both.' 
Eye copies of the two inscriptions have also been prepared under Mr .. Bell's direction; and they have 
been included as Nos. 26 and 27 of the list of inscriptions copied in 1892.

inscnpt10n. About six letters of the first line and three of the second are com-
pletely effaced and some retters seem to be missing at the end of the last line. 
The rest of the record is fairly well preserved. 
The letters are boldly engraved, 
but the rock has not been smoothed before the writing was executed. 
They are, 
on an average, three inches in height, excepting the last sixteen of the fourth line 
and those of the fifth which are about two inches in height. 
The document is not dated ; and there is nothing in its contents that enables 
us to fix its.probable date. A study of its paleography, however, makes it possible 
to ascribe it to the fifth century or thereabouts. 
The script shows a later stage 
of development than that of the ToQigala rock inscription 1 of the third year of 
Sr:imeghavarl.).Qa (circa 362-389 A.D.) as will be evident when the following letters 
occurring in the two epigraphs are placed side by side.

Further examples can be shown to the same effect ; but, on the other hand, the 
letters 1Ja and na in the T 5Qigala record seem to represent a more evolved type 
than those occurring in this inscription. In my article on the T5Qigala inscription, 
I have shown that that record is not altogether representative of the style of 
writing prevailing at the time. 
More developed forms are noticed in records of 
the same reign and those of the reigns of Sr"imeghavarQQa's immediate successors. 
Therefore, the difference in the script should not be taken as indicating a long 
interval between the reign of Srimeghavara.Q.Qa and the date of this inscription. 
The Tissamaharama slab of the reign of Mahana.man 2, on the oth~r hand, shows 
a script further developed than that of this record as will be seen on a comparison 
of the facsimile in Pl. XXIX with plate No. 67 of Muller's A.I.'C. Therefore, 
the present record ought to be ascribed to a date somewhere between the reigns 
of SrimeghavarQQa and Mahana.man. 
The script, on the whole, has a tendency towards curved instead of angular · 
forms; and a large number of the letters slant leftwards. 
The letter ga has 
developed a short horizontal bar at the top. 
The letter u, in line 3, very closely 
. resembles an o; and it is written jostling together with the following letter va. 
E at the beginning of the fourth line. supplies us with an intermediate form

between the angular symbol for that letter found in the second- and third-century 
script and the curvilinear form of the eighth and ninth centuries. 
The same 
line, however, has an e which is more archaic in form. 
The horizontal cross bar 
at the top of the letter na has its two ends curved downwards. 
The two symbols ~ and ~ call for a few remarks. 
The first has been 
noticed in the Palu Makiccava rock inscription of Gajabahu I 1• 
Mr. Bell has 
suggested that 'it may denote the standard by which the value or weight of the 
karshapa~ta was reckoned, i.e. by parlas or quarters' 
2• 
Dr. Wickremasinghe, 
though not quite confident about the significance of this symbol, seems to endorse 
l\lr. Bell's theory. 
The same symbol is found in several other inscriptions dating 
from the early centuries of the present era; for instance, in a rock inscription of 
a king styled Gamiryi Abaya from Vilevava in the Kalpe Kora}e of the Anu-
radhapura District 3, in a rock inscription near the Kiribat-Vehera at Mihintale 
-and in the Ruvanvalisaya pavement inscription of Meghavarl).rya Abhaya. 
In-
variably, the symbol follows the mention of kaliapa~tas; and in the present record, 
as well as in the Vilevava and l\lihintale epigraphs mentioned above, it is followed 
by symbols which most probably are numerical. 
I think there is hardly any 
doubt that the symbol in question indicates the kahapa~za coin itself and the 
figures following it are the numerals expressed already in words. 
In Indian 
inscriptions, there are numerous instances in which numbers already expressed in 
words are given again in symbols. 
The second symbol is a combination of the letters sa and ka. 
We may 
reasonably assume that it expresses the number already given in words, i.e. IOO; 
but it bears a very close resemblance to the symbol for 400 occurring in the 
Gupta inscriptions•. 
We are not quite certain whether the same system of 
numerical symbols that was in vogue in North India, during the early centuries 
of the Christian Era, prevailed in this island also. 
I think it is a reasonable 
explanation to take sa as the initial of the word sata 'hundred' 
and the ka joined 
to it as indicating 'one' as it is the first cohsonant 5• 
The language of this inscription has many points in common with the 
T6Qigala inscription already referred to. 
The contents 
tell us that a certain

REMARKS. 
[Line 1] Ra/iya :-This 
word occurs in other records of about the same 
period; for instance, an inscription at Kaballalel)a, in the Devamadi Hatpattu of 
the Kuru9agala District, mentions a Ratika named Naka 1 and an unpublished 
rock inscription at a place named Burutankanda in the Hambantoia 
District has 
the words, Ra/iya l/,f akayalza put a Ra/iJ 1a Shmyaha. The word ra/iya is derived 
from the P. ratthika (Skt. rii#rika) which occurs in the A 1~5;uttara Nikaya 2 in 
a list of high dignitaries of the state. 
The later Sinhalese equivalent of the word 
was ra/i. 
Maha-ra/i-1zii (the great chief of the riiti) was one of the highest 
officials of the government in the twelfth century 3• A rii/iyii was, evidently,_ the 
officer in charge of a district called ra/a and may have been of equal status to 
the ra/-ladda of the mediaeval inscriptions. 
The term is still preserved in the 
official title Ra/iJmahattayii. In India, corresponding officials were called riiftra-
kii/as; and one of the best known dynasties of the Deccan in mediaeval times 
was known by this name. 
Indian caste-names such as Recfefi have their origin 
in this word and it is interesting to note that in Ceylon, too, a derivative of 
ra/iya, the modern word rate, is a caste designation 4• 
[Line I J Ni/alav1/iya is evidently a village name. 
[Line 2] Sivayi :-Compare, 
for the suffix i, the name Sirinaka-yi in the 
previous inscription.

K ahavatta, see abo\'e p. 2 S 1.

krtvii).

been met with.

tive case plural. Haz•ajara is derived from the Pali smhvacchara (Skt. samvatsara), 
by the elision of the amtsvara, the assimilation of the double consonants, the 
change of the initial s to h and c to j-all 
phonetic changes common in the 
Sinhalese language. 
Later forms of the word ltavajara are havurudzt in the 
mediaeval and classical stages of the language and avurudu which is a very 
common word at the present day. 
The form preserved in the present record is 
of considerable etymological value in that it enables us to connect the modern 
Sinhalese word avurudda with the Vedic sarhvatsara. The interpretation of ana 
is doubtful. 
I have taken it to be equivalent to Skt. anya (Mod. Sin. an) 'other', 
used in a secondary sense denoting futurity. 
With the expression ana havajarana 
in the present record, we may compare ane havijiriya in the Burutankanda rock 
inscription mentioned above, ana havt{jariyi vetf,a ko/aya and havajara ana 
kalahi in two fragmentary 
records from a place named Bac;lagiriya in the

KALUDIYAPOKUNA 
INSCRIPTIONS.

. 
(A.S.1. 
N~s. 429-431). 
Bys. 
PARANAVITANA. 
K
ALUPOKU~A 
or Kaludiyapokui:ia: is the site of an extensive ruined 
monastery situated, on the slopes of a range of hills known as Erava-
lagala, about a mile and a half to the south-east of Kumbukkandanva1a, in the 
Vagapal)aha Pallesiya Pattu of the Mata1e District. 
The principal buildings 
of the monastery, which included a moderate sized stupa and a structure 
supported by massive but roughly hewn stone pillars, were arranged on several 
terraces on the hillside and seem to have been surrounded by a moat. 
As one 
ascends the hill, passing the terraced sites, the ground to the south and the east 
becomes rocky and among the huge granite boulders, there are twelve caves, 
partly artificial, used in ancient days as dwelling-places by Buddhist hermits. 
About 7 50 feet to the south-west of the stiepa, there is a rubble-faced poku~za, the 
muddy water which it is now filled with gives to the whole site its modern name 2• 
1 For a brief description of this site, see C.J. Sc.(§ G) vol. ii, pp. 87-8. 
2 Kaludiyapoku~a is a Sinhalese compound word meaning' Black Water Pool'.

254 
EPIGRAPHIA 
ZEYLANICA 
[VOL. III 
Three inscriptions have been discovered at this place. 
Of these, the 
earliest (No. 431), belonging to about the seventh century A. D., is inscribed on 
a plain guardstone of the flight of steps at the southern entrance to the court-
yard of the stt,pa. 
Only a few letters of this epigraph are legible and we are, 
therefore, unable to say what its contents were. 
The second inscription in point of date (No. 429) is engraved on the rock 
wall of a cave situated about 400 ft. to the south-west of the stupa. 
It is divided 
into five columns of unequal dimensions. 
The first column, containing eighteen 
lines, measures 6 ft. by I ft. 4 in. ; the second of seventeen lines measures 5 ft. 
by I ft. 5 in., and the third, 6 ft. by I ft. 1 in., contains twenty-one lines. 
The 
fourth column measuring I ft. 4 in. by 8 in. contains only six short lines of 
which· the second line consists of one letter, the third of two, and the rest of three 
each. 
The fifth column is engraved in the intervening space between the third 
and the fourth and contains five lines of writing of which the second consists of 
only one letter and the rest of two each. 
It measures 1 ft. 3 in. by 5 in. The 
whole rock surface covered by the inscription measures, roughly, 6 ft. by 6 ft. 
The letters, too, are not of a uniform size ; those in the last eight lines 
of the third column being smaller than the others. 
The average size is about 
two inches in height. 
The engraving of the record is done in a somewhat 
careless manner; the letters being quite shallow and, in many instances, badly 
formed. But, owing to its sheltered position, the record is in a good state 
of preservation. 
· 
The script is that of the ninth century; but is more cursive in form than in 
any of the published epigraphs datable within this period 1• 
Some letters are 
written in more than one type ; for instance, the letter i assumes various shapes 
in lines A 12, A 18, B 2, B 7, C 8, C 13, C 16, C 18, C 21, and E 5. The 
letter gha (line B 8) is found here for the first time in a Sinhalese document of 
the ninth century; in form it closely resembles the symbol for dha. It is difficult 
to distinguish ha and ga from each other owing to the absence, in the former, of 
the hook in its left arm. 
· 
As regards orthography, the mode of writing the two words vibangha and 
samma-sambudu may be noted. 
In the former word, which stands for the 
Skt. vibhanga, the aspirate is transferred from the second to the third syllable,

and the dental n has been substituted for the anusviira. 
In the latter word, 
which is a corruption of P. sammii sambuddha, the use of the conjoint consonants 
is noteworthy. 
Attention may also be drawn to the fact that, in this document, 
the particle yi is added after the finite verb just as the same particle or its variant 
J'a is added in the modern language. 
This particle is, in my opinion, a corruption 
of the 5kt. and P. iti which, in the latter language, is often used to indicate the 
close of a sentence. 
In the long sentence beginning with line A I and ending 
in line B 14 which has several parenthetical clauses, the subject, Da{aniimi, occurs 
twice. 
The first personal termination 
mi is added not only to the finite verh 
(dinmi), but also to the subject Da/anii as well. 
Among the unusual words occurring in this record, mahabundnen in lines 
A Io-
1 1, mutpanin pere bundnen in lines A 12-14, 
miicfabi in line C 1 1 and ag 
in line C 14 call for some remarks here. 
The word bttndna is obscure both in 
its meaning and in its etymology. 
It may, however, be conjectured that it is the 
prototype of mudala 1, meaning 'storehouse' 
or 'treasury'. 
If we adopt this 
hypothesis, mahabund11a, would mean 'the great storehouse'. 
In dealing with 
the Girita!e pillar 2 inscription, I have suggested that pere occurring in technical 
terms of the tenth-century inscriptions may mean 'outer'. 
If this meaning is 
adopted, perebundna would mean 'the outer treasury or storehouse'. 
The con-
text in which the word bundna occurs seems t6 support this conjecture. 
A person 
deposited some money for the daily supply of certain quantities of rice and curd 
to the inmates of a vihara. 
It is reasonable to think that the investment was 
done with the persons who had the management 
of the storehouses of the 
monastery who undertook to supply, in payment of the interest on the invested 
capital, the commodities as requested by the donor. 
As regards the term 
'outer 
storehouse' 
it may be useful to poi~t out that in the monastery of 
Mihinta!e, the income and expenditure were divided into two departments 'the 
interior' and 'the 
exterior' 
3• 
The outer storehouse 
mentioned 
here was 
probably connected with the exterior revenue and expenditure (bahiirz' a;•a viiya). 
Mutpanin may be a place name; if so it should be qualifying perebundna and 
as such, the case ending is noteworthy.

The major part of the

rice should not be given uncooked does not throw much credit on the inmates

It is also laid down that in case of dissension among the 
inmates of the monastery, the food intended for them should be thrown to 
crows and dogs. 
Evidently, Da!ana was of opinion that if the members of the 
saitgha quarrelled amongst themselves, they were less worthy of the offerings of 
the pious than such animals.

worn; but the lower portion, buried in the debris, is fairly well preserved. 
The 
slab, excluding that part which is buried below ground, measures 6 ft. 5 in. by 
2 ft. 4{ in. and contains forty-six lines of writing of which a continuous text can 
be made out from the twenty-ninth line. 
Of the rest, only a few words are 
legible here and there. 
The letters are, on the average, ! in. in height. 
The script belongs to the 
tenth century and is uniform with that of the Vessagiriya Slab-Inscription 
of 
Mahinda IV 1• 
As regards the language, the words membe (line 37), samdndin 
(line 36), k'i~za (line 39), metuvii (line 45), and vii (line 46) are noteworthy. 
Membe is the prototype of moba found in classical Sinhalese writings. 
It appears 
to be a compound of me 'this' 
and be, the locative singular of bii (Skt. bhaga) 
'side', with a nasal introduced between the two words. 
Samandiu is of doubtful 
meaning·; it may be taken as a variant form of sabadin or sambadin (Skt. 
sambandhena)' in connexion with'. 
The etymology and the meaning of ki~za are 
alike doubtful; I have taken it as derived from P. khi~ia, Skt. kfi~ia. Metuvii occurs 
in other places as metuviik. 
The use of the particle vii, in the same sense in which 
it is found used in Pali and Sanskrit, is also met with in later Sinhalese writings 2• 
Had this inscription been completely preserved, it would have been one of the 
most important records of the tenth century. Even in its present fragmentary con-
dition, it is not without some historical interest, for the name of the king who issued 
this edict can be read in line 9, with a reasonable degree of certainty, as Mahasen 
Maharaj. 
His mother's name Vidurarajna, is quite clear; and the name of his 
father may be read, though not with absolute certainty, as Uda. Maharaj. 
It is 
hardly necessary to mention here that the monarch styled Mahasen is not the 
great Mahasena who reigned in the fourth century. 
Maha ('Great') 
is here 
used merely as an epithet and does not form an integral part of the name; and 
therefore, on palaeographical grounds, the monarch who issued this edict must 
be one of the three Sena's who held the sceptre in the tenth century. 
Sena V 
was a son of Mahinda IV 3 ; therefore, he cannot be identical with Mahasen, the 
son of Udamaharaj, 
of this inscription. 
Consequently, 
this epigraph must 
belong to Sena II I or Sena IV. 
But, as the reigns of these two monarchs were 
separated by an interval of only eight years, palaeography c·annot decide as to 
which of them this record must be assigned.

The Mahavamsa does not give the parentage of either of these rulers. 
The 
Pz7/avali, on the other liand, states that Sena I I I was a brother of U daya I I 1 
who was a son of Mihind Maha ya and the princess Kita 2• 
The mother of King 
Sena of our inscription was Vidurarajna and, therefore, he cannot be identified with 
Sena II I. We have, consequently, no other alternative but to identify him with 
Sena IV. 
The next point to be considered is the identity of U damaharaj and 
Vidurarajna, the parents of Sena IV. Of the three kings, named Ucla (Udaya) \ 
figuring in the list of Ceylon's kings, the first of that name died, probably at 
a fairly advanced age, fifty-seven years before the accession of Sena IV; and, 
therefore, it is most unlikely that he was the latter's father. 
The second and 
third monarchs of that name both reigned within twenty years of Sena IV's 
accession and it is one of these two monarchs who has to be identified with 
U clamaharaj, the father of Sena IV and the husband of Vidura. 
The Mahavamsa,(Chap. liii, v. 50) tells us that Udaya III, the predecessor 
of Sena IV, had a wife called Vidura 4. She was, presumably, not Udaya's chief 
consort as, in the chronicle, she is given the epithet orodha (inmate of the harem); 
and this is in accord with the title riijna 5 by which she is referred to in our 
inscnpt1on. 
If, then, we take Vidura, wife of U daya II I, mentioned in the 
chronicle, as identical with the princess Vidura, the wife of U damaharaj 
according to this record, the last named has to be identified with U daya II I. 
This conclusion receives further support from the Pujtivali which tells us 
that Sena who preceded Mahinda IV and who, according to the Mahavanisa, was 
-
the fourth of that name, was a son of his predecessor. 
But the matter is 
complicated by the fact that the Pujavali 6 and, following it, the otlwr Sinhalese

s According to Prof. Geiger's list ( Cii/avamsa, translation, part II, pp. xii-xiii) there were four 
kings named Udaya. 
He has shown that the king named Dappula II in the lists of Wijesinha and

This would necessitate a change in the enumeration of the many Udayas and Dappulas who flourished

historical writings. place two kings, both named Sena, between U daya I I I and 
Mahinda IV 1• 
As U daya II I and Mahinda IV had both the virttda title of 
Sirisangbo 
~ which was borne alternately by the kings of Ceylon during this 
period 3 , these two monarchs could not have been separated from each other by 
two reigns. 
Therefore, we have to conclude that the order of succession of the 
kings from Udaya III to Mahinda IV given in the Jl,,fahavanzsa is to be upheld 
against that of the P17jiivali. 
There is also reason to believe that the insertion, in the P11javalt, of two 
Senas between Udaya III and Mahinda IV is due to a copyist's error. 
The 
passage in question rec:ds :-' His younger brother, king Sen, reigned nine years. 
After him king Uda reigned eight years. 
Afterwards the great king Sen reigned 
nine years. 
His son, the great king Sen, reigned three years' 4• In this passage, 
the number of regnal years attributed 
to the additional Sena who follows 
U daya II I is the same as that given of Sena I II who preceded him and the 
phraseology used is also similar. 
In view of the impossibility, that we have shown 
above, of accomodating two kings between Udaya Ill and Mahinda IV, it is, I 
think, not unreasonable to asume that after the account of U daya I II, the scribe 
repeated, by mistake, the remarks that he had already given of Sena I I I before 
writing down the account of the reign of Sena IV. 
If, then, we delete the sentence 
' Afterwards, the great king Sen reigned nine years' as spurious, the word ' his' 
(ohu) in the next sentence ought to refer to Udaya III; 
and the Pujavali 
can 
thus be understood as stating that Sena IV was a son of U daya I I I-the 
con-
clusion that we have arrived at by the data furnished in the present epigraph 
and in the Mahavamsa. 
Dr. Wickremasinghe, in the genealogical tree facing p. 59 of E.,z., Vol. I I, 
has placed Sena IV as a son of Kassapa V. 
This is due to his assdmption that 
Sena IV was an elder brother of Mahinda IV and that the latter was a son of 
Kassapa V. 
The reasons that he has given for the former assumption (£. Z., 
Vol. I, p. 214) are not conclusive, and I have shown in my article on the Katara-
gama inscriptions 5 that Mahinda IV could not have been a son of Kassapa V.

Therefore,

regarding

Sena IV, according to Dr. Wickremasinghe's 
chronological table, reigned 
from 950 to 953 A.D. 
Though he had a reign of only three· years, he is described 
by the chronicler as a very beneficent ruler ; and had the reputation of being 
a poet. 1 
Our record is a katika, or a set of regulations agreed upon by common 
consent. 
It seems to have consisted of three sections: namely, rules for the 
guidance of the monks, those dealing with the temple officials and regulations to 
be observed by the royal officers in their dealings with the monastery. 
Of these 
three sections, only the second and the third are preserved. 
This inscription 
confirms the previous one as regards the old name of the monastery.

1 Ya/aluma, literally 'hide'. 
1 See E. Z., Vol. I, p. 240, n. 1. 
Dr. Wickremasinghe takes nimi to be a term of land tenure. 
The context here would demand its interpretation either as a term of land tenure or as meaning 'wages' 
or 'payment'. 
The word nimi may be derived from Skt. nivi which means' capital' or 'stock' 
and 
could also have denoted money in general. 
The modern use of the word mudahiffor<ls us a parallel. 
Etymologically it means 'capital' but almost always it denotes money. 
In popular parlance,' wages' 
are \·ery often expressed by a term which means• money'. 
s Katika is the term now used for a set of regulations agreed by the sangha for the guidance of its 
members. 
The word appears to be formed by the addition of the feminine or diminutive form of the 
suffix Ra after the verbal form kala, and therefore would signify ' an Act'. 
Originally it seems to have 
been the term by which a decision of an assembly, whether political or ecclesiastical, was technically 
known. The decisions of the village councils in Malabar are referred to in inscriptions by the cognate 
term kacca (Skt. krtya). 
In the early Pali writings, the word denotes 'agreement', 
'contract' 
or •pact'. 
In the tenth-century Sinhalese, the word had not yet assumed an exclusively ecclesiastical significance 
as proved by the use of the word in the Vessagiriya Slab No. I (E. Z., Vol. I, p. 25).

• Gtdaj, see E. Z., Vol. I, p. 250, n. 5. The exact signific-1nce of this term is still not clear. 
• For the terms ukas and pama1Ju, see Codrington's Short History of Ceylon, p. 46. 
7 Pas-malui-savadda, cf. the 'Jetavanlirlima ', Slab. 
The five most heinous crimes, according to 
Buddhist doctrine are (i) patricide, (ii} matricide, (iii) killing a saint, (iv) causing bodily hurt to the Buddha, 
(v) causing schism in the sangluz. According to Hindu law books, the five great crimes are (i) killing 
a BrahmaJ?.a, (ii} drinking intoxicating liquors, (iii) theft, (iv) committing adultery with the wife of 
a spiritual teacher and (v) associating with any one guiily of these crimes. In Nepal where the ancient 
Indian institutions are still in practice, the five great crimes (paiiciipariidha) are (i) killing a Brahma~a, 
(ii) killing a cow, (iii) killing a woman, (iv) killing a child and (v) acts resulting in loss of caste (see Sylvain 
Levi, Le Nepal, vol. i, pp. 295 ff.). It is impossible to determine how the five great crimes were 
enumerated in medi.-..eval Ceylon.

In Sinhalese inscriptions of this period, the word piirahar and 
its variant forms are used, excepting in this place, in its original meaning of 'pri,ileges •. In the 
modern language, the word perahara, identical in origin, means a procession, usually religious. 
The 
change of meaning in this word as well as the origin of rdigious processions of the sort that we witness 
to-day in Ceylon seem to be due to the periodical exhibition of the privileges, such as the use of 
umbrellas, music, elephants, horses, &c., granted by ancient Sinhalese kings to various religious institu-
tions. 
This is the only instance in which the word is used in this sense in an inscription of 
this period.

PILLAR-INSCRIPTION

By s. PARANAVITANA. 
[VOL. III 
W
HEN this inscribed pillar was brought to the Colombo Museum-from 
where, it is not recorded-it 
was already broken into two pieces. These, 
being kept apart from each other, were treated as two distinct epigraphs by the 
Archaeological Survey when eye-copies and estampages of the inscribed stones 
in the Colombo Museum were prepared in 1907 and 1924. 
The upper part has, 
since then, been further broken into two fragments. 
All the three pieces have 
now been joined together and the complete pillar set up in the stone gallery. 
The pillar is eight inches square and stood seven feet above ground. 
All 
the four sides are inscribed ; A and B contains thirty-two lines each ; C thirty-
three lines a.nd D twelve lines and the figures of a scythe, a monk's fan, a crow 
and a dog. 
The letters are engraved between ruled lines two inches apart from 
one another; and are, on an average, 1} inches in height. 
The mutilation of 
the stone has not done much damage to the inscription which is in .a fair state 
of preservation. 
Excepting certain ak~aras in lines B 21-24 and C 1-4, the 
complete text of the epigraph can be made out with certainty. 
The script does not differ, in any material points, from that of the other 
published epigraphs of Kassapa IV 1, the only noteworthy feature being the 
occurrence of the symbol for jiia in line A 8. 
Several clerical errors are noticed, 
namely, hiti for pihi{i in lines A 3, Karasub for Kasub in li~es A 14-15, the 
repetition of the two ak~aras, eka in lines A I 7-18, niikan for navan 2 in lines A 
23-24, nodana for novadanii in line D 6 and the two superfluous letters vanii in 
line D 8. 
The language and style are the same as in similar documents of the 
period; the only difference to be noted is the more marked tendency to favour 
Sanskrit words instead of genuine vernacular forms. 
Witness, for example, 
ekajiia for eka anna 3, Udaya Abhaya for Uda A bhay 3 and aya for a'· 
The inscription is dated on the tenth day of the first half of the month of

are both derived from the Skt. niiyaka.

Mandindina (February-March) 
in the eleventh year of Kasub Sirisaitgbo, i.e., 
Kassapa IV (circa 891-908 A.D.). The same epithets are applied to this monarch 
in the present epigraph as in the Morago<;la pillar inscription of the sixteenth year 
of that king. 1 
The object of the record is the grant of immunities to an estate which was 
an endowment of a lying-in-home founded by the Chief Secretary 
Senal (Sena). 
In the reign of Kassapa IV, there flourished a Chief Secretary of the name of 
Sena (Seno niima mahalekho, Mv. Ch. lii, v. 33) who was the founder of 
a religious institution at the Mahavihara of Anuradhapura. 
The Mahakalattava 
pillar inscription of the fifteenth regnal year of Sirisangboy (Kassa.pa IV) 2 
mentions the Chief Secretary Sena (Sen Mahala~zan) as the founder of a nunnery, 
called Nalaram in honour of his mother. 
Possibly, the Chief Secretary Sena of 
our inscription is identical with the official mentioned in the chronicle a~d in the 
Mahakalattava 
record. 
The details of the immunities given do not" contain 
anything not known from other grants of a similar nature. 
Six minor officials-dtUakas, as they may be styled-were 
instrumental in 
promulgating this edict. Of these, the first two were members of the king's body-
guard and came under the orders of Kahava Radsivu, the Commander of the 
body-guard. The third and the fourth were from the sabhii itself and came during 
the term of office of Dapu}a Pirittirad, the Sabhapati' (President of the Council). 
The fifth represented the Chief Secretary, Vadura Raksamazia, and the sixth came 
at the instance of Utur Pa9<;lirad who held the office of Deme/a A dhikara. Of the 
higher officials, mentioned above, who had a status corresponding to that of the 
ajnapti in Indian grants 3, two, namely, the Sabhapati and the Demefa Adhikara 
are noticed here for the first time in a Sinhalese document. 
The sabhapati is also mentioned in two other unpublished fragmentary 
inscriptions of the tenth century; one from a place named Dombava]agama in 
the Vilacciya Kora]e of the North-Central Province 4 and the other, belonging to 
the reign of Kassapa IV, from a village called Kuficiku]ama in the Kuficunu 
Kora}e of the same province 5• , A sabhapati or sablziiniiyaka named Kuthara is 
mentioned in the Mal,avamsa 5•

These references do not help us to determine 
the exact nature of the office in question. 
Sabhapatina was one of the highest 
functionaries in the administration of the government as organized by Parakra-
mabahu I 1• 
The office of sabhapati goes back to a great antiquity as it is 
mentioned in the Vedic literature~. 
His function, it is obvious from the name, 
was to preside over the meetings of the Council of State (sabha). 
Our inscrip-
tion, in mentioning the sabltapati of a particular day, seems to indicate that there 
was more than one officer who held this office at the same time. 
Possibly, 
there were two of them who functioned by turns '1• 
The name Pirittirad, 
too, 
is evidently a title. 
In the extract from the Dombava)agama record given above, 
the Sabhapati has the similar title of Pritivude,m 
which seems to be a mispro-
nunciation of the Skt. Prtlwzdeva 'god of the earth' while Pirittirad 
is the same 
as the Skt. Prthvlraja 
'king of the earth·. 
In the Bilibava inscription,• mention 
is made of an official called Sand Pirittirad. 
He is not called the sabhapati in 
that document; but, as in our inscription, some messengers are said to have come, 
for the purpose of proclaiming an edict, from the Council, on the day of his 
presidency. 
He may, therefore, be taken as a holder of the office of sablziipati. 
So far as I know, the office of Deme(ii adhi/.:ari has not been mentioned in 
any other Sinhalese inscription. 
In the chronicles, this office is mentioned but 
once, i.e., in the reign of Parakramabahu 
I 5• 
The expedition sent by that 
monarch to chastise the king of Ramafifia was led by a general named Adicca 
who held the office of Dami'(iidhz'kari. 
Presumably, the Deme(d adhikari figures 
in this edict because the land affected by it contained a Deme(akiibiilla (Tamil 
allotment). 
In my article on the GiritaJe pillar inscription 6, I have given 
references to other documents in which this term occurs. 
On a comparison of 
these, it becomes clear that a Deme(akiibiilla was an allotment of land in a village, 
set apart for the Tamils.

of] Mandindina 3 in the eleventh [year] of His Majesty Kasub Sirisa:ngbo 4 
brother of the great King Udaya Abhaya who, having brought under one rule 
the Ruhu~ r, Province and the Mala District 5, secured for himself the enjoyment 
of the combined wealth, glory and prosperity that prevailed in the beautiful island 
of Lanka r..

1 The word gama (Skt. griima) has hitherto been translated, in almost every place, as 'village'. 
In the present record, the gama in question is said to have consisted of two allotments of land in 
a village named Gai:iagamiya; and, therefore it is clear that the term, in this instance, does not connote 
the idea of' village' in the ordinary sense of that word. Dr. Pran Nath has proved that in Indian documents 
the word grama has frequently the meaning of 'estate ' or ' the area that constituted the territorial unit 
for lhe purpose of revenue assessment' (A Study 1i1 the Economz'c Conditions ef A11cze11t lndz"a, pp. 26-
33). 
This is in keeping with the meaning attached to the word ga111a in Kandyan times. 
D'Oyly, in 
his Sketch ef the Conslz'tution of" the Kan4),an Kingdom (Colombo, 1929, p. 53) says' The Singalese 
word gama properly signifies 'village' ; but, in the Kandyan country is also frequently applied to 
a single estate or a single field, the latter is often called paizguva or share'. 
In colloquial usage, this 
meaning is still attached to the word. 
In many of the edicts of the ninth and tenth centuries, the word 
gama has to be similarly understood.

suffix l or al seems to have had an honorific significance. 
In the proper names Saiigal (P. Sangha, 
see E.Z., Vol. I, p. 42) Kasabal (P. Kassapa, C.J. Sc. G. vol. ii, p. I 17) and Mihzndal (P. JJ/ahinda, 
C. j. Sc. G., vol. ii, p. 117 ), we find the same suffix used precisely in the same manner. 
In the 
modern language, the suffix la is used as an honorific and lii, which appears to be identical in origin, 
denotes the plural number. 
With this it is interesting to compare the Telugu plural suffix lu which 
is also added to proper names as an honorific.

of the Sanskrit f.orm iiya in the present inscription shows that they have to be taken as derivatives of 
the latter and translated 'revenue'. 
On a comparison of these and other similar passages, it seems 
that villages in mediaeval Ceylon were classified according to the head-quarters at which they paid their 
tevenue. In this instance, Valvit, appears to have been the head-quarters of a group of villages in which 
Ga9agami was also included.

tentative explanation of this word, see ~.bove, p. 107.

at Anuradhapura. 
This irregularity in spelling is no doubt due to the fact that Skt. kabala, from which 
it is derived, has the variant form of kabarja. The original meaning of the word is 'mouthful'; 
but in

the conjunction isii have been treated as independent sentences. In the translation given above, I have 
treated the whole sentence to be more in keeping with the construction of the original.

T
HE sub-joined inscription is engraved on a slab, measuring 
I ft. 7 in. by 
5 ft., now set up near the Bo-tree in the Buddhist temple at Dadigama 1 in 
the Beligal Kora!e of the Kagalla District. 
The stone has been broken into 
two fragments which are now joined together. 
The inscription is engraved on 
both faces of the slab; side A contains seventeen lines of writing and occupies 
an area of 4 ft. 8 in. by I ft. 5 in. and the inscribed space in side B measures 
3 ft. 6 in. by I ft. 5 in. and contains twenty-one lines. 
The letters, engraved 
between lines abou; two inches apart from one another, are, on an average, one 
inch in height, excepting those of the first line in side A, which are somewhat 
larger. 
The script which differs very little from the modern, calls for no remarks 
in particular. 
The language 
is Sinhalese and, in accordance with the style in 
vogue during the period, contains a good number of Sanskrit tatsmas in the 
writing of which, however, the rules of Sanskrit orthography are not strictly 
adhered to. 
Witness, for example, vamsotbhiita and dik-vijaya which would, in 
Sanskrit, be correctly written as vamsodbhiUa and dzj[-vijaya. 
The inscription is dated on the thirteenth day of the waxing moon in the 
month of Poson in the ninth year of Bhuvanekabahu (the sixth) whose initial 
regnal year, according 
to Dr. Wickremasinghe's 
Chronological 
table, was 
1 Dadigama was of some -political importance during the middle of the fourteenth century, for 
Parakramabahu V, the prince who exercised joint authority with Bhuvanekabahu IV of Gampola, 
resided there (E.. Z., Vol. Ill, p. 30) 
The Pali poem Vullanuila and the Sinhalese Tisara Sandesa 
have conventional descriptions of Darligama when this town was the capital of a king named 
Parakramabahu who, in all probability, was Parakramabahu V. 
Some scholars are of opinion that 
Parakramabiihu of Dadigama mentioned in these two works was the prince named Parakrama A.pa who 
had a brief tenure of authority just before the accession of Parakramabahu VI of Koge (See Ca. vol. i, 
pp. 48 ff.). Dadigama seems to have been of some note before this period, too; as there is, at the 
place, a large ruined diigiiba now known as Kota1·ehera. 
Mr. Codrington proposes to identify this 
, diigiiba with the Siitighara Cetiya built at Purhkhagama by Pariikramabahu the Great to mark the site 
of his birth place (see C.J. Sc. § G, vol. ii, p. I 35). 
The antiquity of Dadigama is carried still further 
back 'by the fragment of a tenth-century pilla-r-inscription recently brought to notice (A. S. I. No. 57).

wrested from its Tamil kings.

VI, his

the throne himself.

VI; 
while the later chroniclers

Kotte.

part of the country had not yet been settled when this epigraph was written. 
The king's desire not to show undue severity towards the inhabitants of the Four 
Korajas was most probably dictated by the necessity of his securing the co-opera-
tion of these people in his plans for subduing the mountainous districts. 
The 
people seemed to have entertained the fear that the king's conciliatory attitude 
would last only till the subjugation of the Uc;laraia had been accomplished; hence 
their anxiety to have the king's amnesty for their past rebellious conduct ensured 
by means of this edict. 
[Lines B 2-3] SiJhha/a-satizl{e :- The word smizga, in Sanskrit, has the 
meaning of' war' or 'battle'; 
hence, the compound Si1ilha(a-sa1j~![cl 
would mean 
'the Sinhalese war'. 
It seems certain that this word is synonymous with 
Si1i1hala-pera(iya (the Sinhalese insurrection), the name given, in the Raja,1alt1, 
to a serious rebellion against the authority of Bhuvanekabahu 
by the people of 
the country between the Kaluganga and the Valave Ga11ga under the leadership 
of Srivardhana Patiraja and Kurugama Himi. The rebellion, headed by a person 
called Garavi, which was raging at Valigama when the Burmese mission was 
forced to land there 2, seems to have been an episode of this wide spread rising. 
\Ve learn from this inscription that the people of the Four Koralas, too, were 
against the king during the course of this civil commotion. 
When the southern 
parts of the island raised the standard of revolt, the prince of Arnbulugala, 
the ruler of the Four Kora)as, was dispatched by the king to subdue the 
insurgents. 
1-1 
is absence seems to have been taken advantage of by the people 
of the Four Koralas, in their turn, to rise in revolt. 
It also appears that the 
U c;larata, too, had declared against the king at the same time. 
Thus it is evident 
that all the Sinhalese people, excepting those in the immediate vicinity of his 
capital, rose against Bhuvanekabahu. 
The name given to the rebellion suggests 
that it was a national rising against Bhuvanekabahu and his family who were of 
South Indian origin. 
Just as he did in the case of the refractory people of the 
Four Kora)as, Bhuvanekabahu adopted a conciliatory attitude to the leaders 9f 
the Simhala-pera/iya; 
for when they were brought before him as captives, he 
contented 
himself with punishing them with imprisonment. 
Shortly after, 
he not only released them, but also placed his adopted son and heir under their 
protection a. 
We do not know whether this attitude of the king was due to his

phraseology 
occurs in an unpublished inscription at Ga<;laladeQ.iya, granting amnesty, by a king

[Line B 7] 1Va(iira appears to be the earlier form of niarii(a which occurs 
in copper plate inscriptions of the period 1• The form ma(ara also occurs in an 
unpublished rock inscription, at Ga<;laladeQ.iya, of a king named Senasammata 
Vikramabahu, where we read iitva,_1;e 
duva vii/u~w kenakunge ma(iiraya/a himi 
kenek niituva tibe nam fariivasava tibcna vihara karavima/a pudanuvat (if there 
be no person entitled to the ma(ara of a person who had fallen whilst running in 
elephant hunts, the same may be dedicated for the repair of dilapidated 
monaiteries}. 
The word ma(iira, most probably, is derived from Skt. mrta 
' dead' and hara 'what is taken ' and would etymologically mean ' what is taken 
from dead persons·. 
According to Sinhalese institutions, when a person died 
all his movable properties passed to the king if he had no male heir; otherwise, 
one-third of it belonged to the king. 
The custom was in vogue during the 
Portuguese period in the territories under their rule. 
For a description of the 
institution in actual practice, see Dr. Pieris's, Ceylon, the Portuguese Era, vol. ii, 
pp. 80--2. 
In this particular instance, the ma(iira of those persons of the Four 
Kora1as who had acted treasonably would have been confiscated by the king, 
but in pursuance of the policy of reconciliation, already noted, the king was 
satisfied with only a half share. 
[Lines B 8-1 2] The forfeiture of estates referred to here must have been 
in connexion with those who had taken a leading part in the recent rebellion. 
Leaving one amu~a of paddy land, a house and a garden-just 
enough property to 
maintain one's livelihood-to 
the original owner must have been considered an 
act of clemency on the king's part. Ordinarily, the entire property of a rebel was 
confiscated. 
[Lines 12-16] Invoking the authority of the gods and of the sacred 
objects held in the highest veneration by the Sinhalese kings was doubtless to 
make the edict binding on the part of the monarch.

to the close resemblance 
between the symbols for ha and nga, this name has been read both in this

A PILLi\R 
INSCRIPTION 
OF BHUVANAIKABAHU 
MAHAPA 
FROM ANURADHAPURA. 
By S. PAR.\NAVITANA. 
T
HIS inscription is the latest in elate amo~g the records of.Sinhalese royalty 
so far found at Anuradhapura. 
It was discovered, sometime between 1906 
antl 1912, in a chena near the Malva tu Oya, to the east of the fifth milestone 
on the Outer Circular Road; and has been included as No. 2 in the list of 
inscriptions forming Appendix F of the Annual 
Report of the Archaeolop;ical 
Survey of Ceylon for 1911-12. 
The pillar on which it is engraved, now preserved 
in the Anuradhapura l\'Iuseum, has evidently been taken from the ruins of an 
ear1ier building. 
The lower portion of the pillar has been broken off and, at its 
top, there is a mortise hole to which a wooden capital was probably fitted. 
Two 
of its sides measure 71 inches each and the other two sides 7i inches and 7{ 
inches respectiveiy. 
Its present height is 4 ft. s½ 
in. 
The- inscription consists 
of nine lines of writing on side A, covering a space of 1 ft. 7½ in. by 7; in. and

The script is of the thirteenth

Vijayabahu I II 1 •

acknowledged in those parts of the island.

brother.

the son of Parakramabahu 
II, is given 
in the

Hatthigiripura.

again at the ever to be honoured spot where his corpse was laid (on the pyre),

impermanency.

house and had made for it a great image of the Buddha. 
Then having had

ornaments.

HE first of the two short inscriptions dealt with in this article comes from 
a village named Kivuleka<;la in the Kuficuttu Kora)e of the North-Central 
Province. 
It has been noticed, for the first time, by Mr. Bell in his A 1mual 
Report for the year 1892, p. 7, where he says:-' 
Kivuleka(.la ;-one 
and a half 
miles from Ayitigev<;wa. 
Here is an inscribed pillar slab, short and very rough. 
The Arachchi stated that it was below ground, except one corner, and that the 
inscription was discovered when he had the stone dug out for a support to his 
a/uva (granary). 
About eleven lines of writing are visible ; above are Buddhist 
emblems. 
The record belongs to the ninth century and a king styled' Rilz:r:ala 
Purmkdnt 
Sa!amevan who may be identified as Silamegha, or Sena I (844-
866 A.D.) '. 
When I visited Kivuleka<;la in 1928, the Aracci mentioned by Mr. Bell and his 
a/uva wer«:? both no more; and the pillar was lying, half-buried, on the ground with 
the inscribed face downwards, near the spill of the Kll<;la Kivuleka<;la tank by the 
· side of the footpath leading to the village of Maha Kivuleka<;la. As has been stated, 
the pillar is extremely rough; and the engraving has been done in a very clumsy 
manner. 
The inscribed portion measures 3 ft. by I ft. 2 in. and contains 11_ lines 
of writing. 
At the top are auspicious symbols; a flower vase (piir1J~ia-gha/a), 
a crescent, a lotus, a trisu!a, a svastika, and three others which are not recog-
nizable. 
A few letters in the last three lines are obliterated ; the rest of the 
record is in a fairly good state of preservation. 
The script does not differ, in 
essential points, from that of the inscriptions of Sena I I, Udaya I and Kassapa IV. 
The letter a in the first line is somewhat unusual in form and can easily be mis-· 
taken for a pu, as has actually been done by Mr. Bell. 
As regards grammar. the verbal forms vaja(eyin and avu_j occurring in 
this record appear as vadii(eyin and avud in the later stages of the language. 
The form kiiru is interesting; 
it is obviously an earlier form of karu, occurring 
in the standard Sinhalese speech, and is derived from Skt. kiira 'doer'. 
It is 
also possible to take it as a variant form of kiiru (P. karita). 
The inscription refers itself to a king named Salamevan, 
'the founder of the 
Ritigal monastery'. 
Ritigala is mentioned in the Mahava1itsa by the name of

succeeding reigns had not yet been developed.

The second inscription that can be ascribed to the reign of Sena I is on 
a pillar found used in the pavement of the Vata-da-ge at Po1onnarnva, and now 
preserved in the Archaeological Museum at Anuradhapura. 
It is included as 
No. 55 in the list of inscriptions examined by the Archaeological Survey between 
190 I and 1905. 
The pillar is not well dressed and its sides are not regular. 
Roughly, it measures, including the rounded capital at the top, 9 in. by 7½ in. by 
6~ ft. in height. 
The inscription covers only one side of the pillar; on the second 
face, there is a rude drawing of a man, standing, holding a club in one of his 
hands and on the third face are drawings of a dog and a crow. Only twelve lines 
of the inscription are preserved, the rest having been wilfully damaged, possibly 
when this pillar was used as a paving stone. 
Above the first line, remnants of 
two decorative designs between parallel lines are -yisible. The letters, about 
2 in. in height, are engraved fairly deep; and in the preserved portion of the 
inscription, they are clearly legible. 
The script is of the ninth century and individual letters do not call for any 
remarks. 
We may, however, draw attention to the fact that the medial vowel 
signs for ii, e and o, which are detached in the script from about the time of 
Sena II, downwards, are here written attached to the consonant. 
This feature 
is noticed in the script of the early period and continued to about the beginning 
of the ninth century. 
As regards graJJJ.mar, the two forms Ase/ii and visevilli 
for the usual Ase/a and viseni are noteworthy. 
The record is dated in the fifteenth year of a king styled Abha Salamevan. 
Mr. Bell has taken the regnal year given in this epigrap\1 as the forty-fifth, and 
referring to it he says:-' 
The short pillar record recovered from the pavement 
of the T¥a/a-dd-ffe necessitates emendment in the length of reign assigned by Sin-
halese histories to some king of the 8th or 9th centuries'. 
'The inscription is 
dated in th~ 45th year of the reign of an "Abhii Salamevan ", According to the
